{"parentUuid":"e1b83033-33a9-42b5-94b6-8564eb592eeb","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"c7f62f57-e583-41df-8c11-b94276aef657","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01HbDLoSjeVJGo4e6X1Bd4ZY","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Territory Studio Blade Runner 2049 UI typography design process logomark\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Blade Runner 2049 - Territory Studio\",\"url\":\"https://territorystudio.com/project/blade-runner-2049/\"},{\"title\":\"Blade Runner 2049 - UI Design — HUDS+GUIS\",\"url\":\"https://www.hudsandguis.com/home/2018/blade-runner-2049\"},{\"title\":\"Blade Runner 2049 | Cantina Creative | Design & VFX\",\"url\":\"https://www.cantinacreative.com/film/blade-runner-2049\"},{\"title\":\"Territory Studio - BladeRunner 2049 UI\",\"url\":\"https://sheldonhicks.artstation.com/projects/JlmP9m\"},{\"title\":\"BLADE RUNNER 2049 - SCREEN GRAPHICS & UI DESIGN - Andrew Popplestone\",\"url\":\"https://www.behance.net/gallery/63113211/BLADE-RUNNER-2049-SCREEN-GRAPHICS-UI-DESIGN\"},{\"title\":\"Pinterest\",\"url\":\"https://www.pinterest.com/pin/460000549434492489/\"},{\"title\":\"A visual journey through the screen graphics of ‘Blade Runner 2049’\",\"url\":\"https://vfxblog.com/2017/11/08/a-visual-journey-through-the-screen-graphics-of-blade-runner-2049/\"},{\"title\":\"Q&A with David Sheldon-Hicks, founder of Territory Studio - Design Museum\",\"url\":\"https://designmuseum.org/exhibitions/past-exhibitions/beazley-designs-of-the-year-2018/qa-with-david-sheldon-hicks-founder-of-territory-studio\"},{\"title\":\"Blade Runner 2049 Screen Graphics - Territory Studio\",\"url\":\"https://www.behance.net/gallery/58693247/Blade-Runner-2049-Screen-Graphics\"},{\"title\":\"Territory Studio: Blade Runner 2049 – Cospective\",\"url\":\"https://cospective.com/news/territory-studio/\"}]\n\nHere's a comprehensive overview of **Territory Studio's design process for *Blade Runner 2049*** — covering UI, typography, logomarks, and more:\n\n---\n\n## 🎬 Territory Studio & *Blade Runner 2049*: Design Process\n\n### 🏢 Early Involvement & Brief\nTerritory Studio was tapped to create screen graphics for *Blade Runner 2049*, joining the art department in the very early stages of production. They worked closely with Director Denis Villeneuve and Supervising Art Director Paul Inglis to support storybeats, performance, and context.\n\nApart from on-set screen graphics, there was no clear brief. Instead, Creative Director Andrew Poplestone and Creative Lead Peter Eszenyi traveled to Budapest to speak with Paul Inglis and Denis Villeneuve, who outlined the film's themes and the state of the Blade Runner universe 30 years after the original.\n\n---\n\n### 🔤 Typography & Logomark Design\nWith an open brief to create logos and signage, the team had fun inventing colourful brands, characters, icons, and typographic treatments, drawing on **kanji, Latin, Cyrillic, and Arabic letterforms**.\n\n---\n\n### 🧪 Experimental & Physical Design Process\nAsked to explore physical and organic alternatives to digital, LED, and devices, the team sought to develop concepts that felt seamless with the bleak world of 2049. Setting aside CG tools and sketchbooks, they turned to the physical textures of old-school technology — spending the first weeks experimenting with optical lenses, cine projectors, microfiche, and card systems. Even grapes, grapefruits, eyeballs, and bone were dissected, magnified, photographed, and scanned to create organic abstraction.\n\nAs creatives with an art school and graphic design background, they often start projects with a blank page, pens, and pencils. The difference for this project was that Director Denis Villeneuve was so enthusiastic about originality beyond digital conventions that he encouraged experimentation.\n\n---\n\n### 🖥️ UI Design Language & Screen Systems\nMarrying narrative content and textural layers in CG, the team designed and animated each specific story beat, creating original interface and interaction systems that felt **authentic and credible** within the context of each specific scene.\n\nWorking across **15 sets**, they delivered over **100 assets** for on-set playback, also driving the design direction for concepts delivered to post-production.\n\n---\n\n### 🚗 Character-Driven Interface Design (K's Spinner)\nTo convey age and disrepair, they designed interfaces with **warping, ghosting, and colour degradation**, adding glitches and surface textures to suggest out-of-date technology that had seen a long and rough life.\n\nThe director and Paul Inglis were clear that this was a new world, 30 years on from the original, so the technology had to reflect that evolution. At the same time, it was important that 2049 felt like a natural evolution, so they drew parallels in the physicality of the tech, colour palettes, and screen types — acknowledging that the technology had some **regressive qualities due to the blackout event**.\n\n---\n\n### 🌊 Inspiration & Mood Boards\nFor founder David Sheldon-Hicks, avoiding clichés was critical: *\"Sci-fi UI is often criticized when everything is just turned blue.\"* The team developed mood boards drawing from **opera, dance choreography, luminous sea life, and more**, allowing them to be more playful with the palette and impressionistic with UI design.\n\n---\n\n### 🏆 Recognition\nTerritory Studio's work was nominated for the **Beazley Designs of the Year** exhibition at the Design Museum, with their fictional technology screens for *Blade Runner 2049* appearing alongside real-world projects across all design disciplines.\n\n---\n\nFor more, you can explore Territory Studio's official project page at [territorystudio.com](https://territorystudio.com/project/blade-runner-2049/) or their detailed Behance showcase.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"3dc1060a-edd9-4b5b-b43c-61c76938ed0f","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:15:12.146Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"e1b83033-33a9-42b5-94b6-8564eb592eeb","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

{"parentUuid":"4a50512a-5f3e-4feb-9668-bd182f8866fc","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"c7f62f57-e583-41df-8c11-b94276aef657","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Te9RHozV4J9KhMsniQiKXd","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Ash Thorp Ghost in the Shell title design typography process cyberpunk\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Ghost in the Shell - Ash Thorp\",\"url\":\"https://www.altcinc.com/work/gits\"},{\"title\":\"Main Title Design | Ash Thorp | Learn Squared\",\"url\":\"https://www.learnsquared.com/courses/main-title\"},{\"title\":\"GHOST IN THE SHELL :: Behance\",\"url\":\"https://www.behance.net/gallery/51424063/GHOST-IN-THE-SHELL\"},{\"title\":\"Ghost in the Shell FUI Design — HUDS+GUIS\",\"url\":\"https://www.hudsandguis.com/home/2017/4/17/ghostintheshell-fui\"},{\"title\":\"Motionographer® Ash Thorp: Project 2501, Homage to Ghost in the Shell [NSFW]\",\"url\":\"https://motionographer.com/2014/05/27/ash-thorp-project-2501-homage-to-ghost-in-the-shell/\"},{\"title\":\"A Look at the Process Behind Ghost in the Shell's Vision of the Future\",\"url\":\"https://gizmodo.com/a-look-at-the-process-behind-ghost-in-the-shells-vision-1794046269\"},{\"title\":\"Ash Thorp\",\"url\":\"https://www.pinterest.com/ideas/ash-thorp/935699248703/\"},{\"title\":\"Solograms of Ghost in The Shell | Ghost in the shell, Ghost, Cyberpunk aesthetic\",\"url\":\"https://www.pinterest.com/pin/31314159895964989/\"},{\"title\":\"Project 2501 - Ash Thorp\",\"url\":\"https://www.altcinc.com/work/project-2501\"},{\"title\":\"Ghost In The Shell — Vitaly Bulgarov\",\"url\":\"https://vitalybulgarov.com/ghost-in-the-shell/\"}]\n\nHere's a comprehensive overview of what the search results reveal about **Ash Thorp's work on *Ghost in the Shell***, spanning title design, typography, and the cyberpunk creative process:\n\n---\n\n### 🎨 Ash Thorp & *Ghost in the Shell*\n\n**Who is Ash Thorp?**\nAsh Thorp is a director, designer, and illustrator whose boundary-pushing work spans film, games, and animation. He contributed to major projects such as *Ghost in the Shell*, *Blade Runner 2049*, *Ender's Game*, *Call of Duty*, and *Assassin's Creed*.\n\n---\n\n### 🌆 The Cyberpunk Visual World\n\nIn a reel posted to Vimeo, Ash Thorp catalogued some of the work he did to help create *Ghost in the Shell*'s particular look, with an accompanying post on his website going more in-depth, showing how the movie's solid hologram effects came to life.\n\nEarly on in the film's development, Thorp met with director Rupert Sanders to discuss the creative direction. Sanders expressed his desire to paint the city with neon lights in a new form he coined as **\"Solograms\"** — solid holograms that exist in the realm of a particle system of light that can be moved and augmented in Z space. Thorp loved the idea and immediately got to work building out concepts.\n\nThe film takes place in a world full of holograms and cyborgs, with the city absolutely saturated in holograms — from eye-catching advertising to street signs and road signals. Director Rupert Sanders referred to them as \"Solograms.\" The abundance of hologram advertising taking over buildings makes the world feel artificial, with one foot in reality and the other in virtual reality.\n\n---\n\n### 🛠️ The Creative Process & Tools\n\nThorp's work included a mix of various style frames, concepts, and final production assets that made it into the film. These concepts then went into post-production, where Chris Bjerre and Thorp animated and created an asset library for director Rupert Sanders to \"paint his city with.\"\n\nThe amazing elements Thorp constructed for the film were built using **Cinema 4D**.\n\nWhen time or resources for a large photoshoot aren't available, realistic 3D can be a wonderful substitution in title design. Thorp's process involves using **Cinema 4D, Octane, and Marvelous Designer** in these situations.\n\nThorp's philosophy for creating a title sequence is rooted in graphic design, revisiting the world of **typography**, building out final composites in **Photoshop**, and learning to step back and reflect on the work.\n\n---\n\n### 🎬 Project 2501 — Reimagining the Original Anime Title Sequence\n\nBefore working on the live-action film, Ash Thorp teamed up with photographer **Tim Tadder** and over 20 artists to reimagine the title sequence of the classic anime film *Ghost in the Shell*. The making-of and full set of boards were viewable on the Project 2501 site.\n\nFor a film that was a comment on the uber-connected society of the future, Project 2501 itself became an example of how a collaborative group of creatives can be so deeply connected.\n\n---\n\n### 👥 Collaborators on the Live-Action Film\n\nThe live-action *Ghost in the Shell* film (directed by Rupert Sanders) featured Ash Thorp as a **Concept Artist/Designer**, alongside Lead Concept Artists **Maciej Kuciara** and **Vitaly Bulgarov**, Designer **Chris Bjerre**, and Reel Editor **Franck Deron**.\n\nThe FUI (Fictional User Interface) work on the film was a collaboration between **MPC**, **Territory Studios**, and **Ash Thorp**.\n\n---\n\n### 📚 Teaching the Craft\n\nThorp has also taught the art of title sequence design through Learn Squared. He walks students through various examples and dissects what works and what doesn't in title sequences — studying how the greats are able to capture a unique glimpse of a story in a short amount of time.\n\n---\n\nAsh Thorp's work on *Ghost in the Shell* is a landmark example of **cyberpunk visual design**, blending typography, holographic UI, and intricate 3D worldbuilding into a cohesive futuristic aesthetic.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"f80caf60-3d0b-4946-9e12-93cc41f9b423","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:15:47.137Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"4a50512a-5f3e-4feb-9668-bd182f8866fc","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

{"parentUuid":"0dfdc0da-e686-4774-ae0c-320872b3cf87","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"progress","data":{"type":"query_update","query":"GMUNK typography design process sci-fi UI Tron Legacy Oblivion brand identity"},"toolUseID":"search-progress-1","parentToolUseID":"toolu_01MZDMhUFeo9qfwdbHfFKiBS","uuid":"3b4f29fd-bfc1-42f6-bd3b-11924c46cca7","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:15:50.747Z","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}
{"parentUuid":"3b4f29fd-bfc1-42f6-bd3b-11924c46cca7","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"progress","data":{"type":"search_results_received","resultCount":10,"query":"GMUNK typography design process sci-fi UI Tron Legacy Oblivion brand identity"},"toolUseID":"srvtoolu_015upMtZ3j2WefMZut3irDNH","parentToolUseID":"toolu_01MZDMhUFeo9qfwdbHfFKiBS","uuid":"5f7a8561-e736-4909-9de6-1c654fe5d966","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:15:52.692Z","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}
{"parentUuid":"0dfdc0da-e686-4774-ae0c-320872b3cf87","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"c7f62f57-e583-41df-8c11-b94276aef657","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01MZDMhUFeo9qfwdbHfFKiBS","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"GMUNK typography design process sci-fi UI Tron Legacy Oblivion brand identity\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"UI Review: Oblivion – INTERFACE LOVE.\",\"url\":\"https://ilikeinterfaces.com/2015/04/21/ui-review-oblivion/\"},{\"title\":\"“Crushing” the User Interface Designs of 'Oblivion'\",\"url\":\"https://www.awn.com/vfxworld/crushing-the-user-interface-designs-of-oblivion\"},{\"title\":\"Oblivion GFX — GMUNK\",\"url\":\"https://gmunk.com/OBLIVION-GFX\"},{\"title\":\"Motionographer® Bradley G Munkowitz: Oblivion Screen Graphics\",\"url\":\"https://motionographer.com/2013/04/19/bradley-g-munkowitz-oblivion-screen-graphics/\"},{\"title\":\"HUD and UI graphics in 'Oblivion' + titles by Rich Young - ProVideo Coalition\",\"url\":\"https://www.provideocoalition.com/hud-and-gui-graphics-in-oblivion/\"},{\"title\":\"ARTICLE\",\"url\":\"https://fsumm.github.io/Project4/ui-review-oblivion.html\"},{\"title\":\"Oblivion - Interface Design — HUDS+GUIS\",\"url\":\"https://www.hudsandguis.com/home/2013/05/02/oblivion-interface-design\"},{\"title\":\"Information — GMUNK\",\"url\":\"https://gmunk.com/Information\"},{\"title\":\"OBLIVION GFX - GMUNK ™\",\"url\":\"https://www.behance.net/gallery/49675041/OBLIVION-GFX?locale=en_US\"},{\"title\":\"Pinterest\",\"url\":\"https://www.pinterest.com/pin/450852612667748208/\"}]\n\nHere's a comprehensive overview of **GMUNK's typography, design process, and sci-fi UI work** across *Tron: Legacy* and *Oblivion*, along with his broader brand identity:\n\n---\n\n## 🎨 Who Is GMUNK?\n\n**Bradley G. Munkowitz**, known professionally as **GMUNK**, is a renowned design director and motion artist. He pioneered early Augmented Reality concepts by designing the holographic content in science-fiction pillars *Tron: Legacy* and *Oblivion*. His commercial work has been prolific, with commissions for clients such as Apple, Disney, Nike, Adidas, Samsung, Microsoft, Meta, Audi, NASA, Sony, Adobe, and HBO. His work often centers around themes of identity, the subconscious, and our human connection to technology.\n\n---\n\n## 🎬 Tron: Legacy\n\n*Tron* was all about three-dimensional holograms, while *Oblivion* was all about elegant user interfaces of the future. On *Tron*, GMUNK's team didn't do much UI work, but what they did was all weird, dataviz-type 3D holograms.\n\nThe interface screens used by Victoria on the light table in *Oblivion* were designed by Bradley G. Munkowitz, who had previously collaborated with Kosinski while at **Digital Domain** on *TRON: Legacy* graphics.\n\n---\n\n## 🎬 Oblivion — Design Process & UI\n\n### The Team\nJoseph Kosinski, who studied architecture, directed his second film *Oblivion* after *Tron: Legacy*. Kosinski worked with Producer Steve Gaub and Lead Interface Graphic Designer Bradley Munkowitz (better known as GMUNK), both of whom were also involved in the director's previous project. GMUNK assembled a small team of super awesome graphic designers and motion artists that operated as an independent team working directly with Kosinski.\n\nJake Sargeant and Joseph Chan were brought on as graphic designers, and David Lewandowski (Dlew), Navarro Parker, and Alexander Perry did motion.\n\n### Design Philosophy\nThe briefing for the graphic language stressed **functionality and minimalism** while utilizing a bright, unified color palette that would appear equally well on both a dark or bright backdrop. The function was to reflect the modernized sensibilities of the TET Mainframe computer and would assist the characters with the key components of their duties on Earth — be it the monitoring of all vitals on the ground using Vika's Light Table, or the various diagnostics in the air using the Bubbleship.\n\n### Grid & Consistency\nGMUNK explained the use of a **dot grid** to hang all the design elements on. It really helped create a neat, consistent, structured system throughout the film's UI. The overall style and language of the UI was created by the type, icons, colour palette, and use of thin details.\n\nThe graphical style of the movie is unified by the use of a clean and organized grid system. GMUNK and his team kept a consistency throughout the UI. The thin sharp lines, clean compositions, and the fact that everything aligns so nicely to the grid system makes everything feel futuristic and slick.\n\n### Scope of Work\nMunkowitz and his team oversaw the production of assets for scores of contexts, including an **interactive light table**, cockpit elements for the \"Bubbleship,\" and HUD elements for various equipment and weapons in the film.\n\nThe HUD and UI graphics were mostly shot in-camera after being created by a team led by Bradley G. Munkowitz, aka GMUNK.\n\n---\n\n## 🔤 Typography\n\nThe main UI font of *Oblivion* is called **Blender**, one not commonly seen used in sci-fi films before. The font draws clear inspirations from **Futura** (which ties together with the narrative since Futura is one of NASA's official fonts) and **Stop** (a font created by Aldo Novarese, who also created the most famous sci-fi font Eurostile, used in films like *2001: A Space Odyssey*, *Moon*, etc.).\n\nApart from being used in the UI, the font is also used on the stations, drones, and rigs.\n\n---\n\n## 🎨 Color Palette\nMost of the UI in *Oblivion* uses low-saturated blues and oranges, a typical color palette for sci-fi movie interfaces. The team kept most colors desaturated and used saturated colors for warnings, alerts, important symbols/messages, and zoning of dangerous areas.\n\n---\n\n## 🏆 Influence & Legacy\n\nGMUNK has stated that from a usability standpoint, *Minority Report* is \"still the king,\" and he doesn't know when it will ever be outdone. His team has seen every sci-fi movie with a good interface design in it and knows all the top UI designers in the world — their reference library is incredibly vast.\n\nGMUNK also produced one of the world's most iconic image campaigns with the **Microsoft Windows 10 desktop wallpaper**, seen by over a billion people worldwide.\n\n---\n\nGMUNK's work on *Tron: Legacy* and *Oblivion* remains some of the most celebrated sci-fi UI design in cinema, blending typographic precision, grid-based systems, and a deeply considered design language that feels both futuristic and functional.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"f71c33b9-8363-4ec8-9971-6d19a07bc9b9","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:16:29.251Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"0dfdc0da-e686-4774-ae0c-320872b3cf87","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

{"parentUuid":"73dcdeae-713c-48e5-a1a1-6464139dc447","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"progress","data":{"type":"search_results_received","resultCount":10,"query":"Perception design studio Avengers UI typography sci-fi interface design process"},"toolUseID":"srvtoolu_01DGPncgjJCJHY5FSvq3tzwE","parentToolUseID":"toolu_01Y4X9H3KoBeqAzWBkcgtCSV","uuid":"126a754a-e100-41bd-8845-6740e651c463","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:16:34.521Z","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}
{"parentUuid":"66015413-8f28-408b-9a26-736eb8304ec7","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"6c2d9219-4df4-497c-86ad-e2941100841a","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Y4X9H3KoBeqAzWBkcgtCSV","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Perception design studio Avengers UI typography sci-fi interface design process\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"The Design Firm Behind Those Futuristic User Interfaces in the Marvel Cinematic Universe - Core77\",\"url\":\"https://www.core77.com/posts/108437/The-Design-Firm-Behind-Those-Futuristic-User-Interfaces-in-the-Marvel-Cinematic-Universe\"},{\"title\":\"Sci-Fi Graphics Are Influencing Real-World UI Design | Built In\",\"url\":\"https://builtin.com/articles/sci-fi-ui\"},{\"title\":\"Perception — Art of the Title\",\"url\":\"https://www.artofthetitle.com/studio/perception/\"},{\"title\":\"Sci-fi interfaces and emerging technology - Territory Studio\",\"url\":\"https://territorystudio.com/sci-fi-interfaces-and-emerging-technology-4/\"},{\"title\":\"UI Design in Marvel's 'Avengers - Infinity War ' — HUDS+GUIS\",\"url\":\"https://www.hudsandguis.com/home/2018/avengers-infinity-war\"},{\"title\":\"Avengers Technology Design | PERCEPTION\",\"url\":\"https://www.experienceperception.com/work/the-avengers-tech-design/\"},{\"title\":\"Sci-fi Interfaces Q&A with Perception Studio | Sci-fi interfaces\",\"url\":\"https://scifiinterfaces.com/2021/04/13/sci-fi-interfaces-qa-with-perception-studio/\"},{\"title\":\"Experience Perception\",\"url\":\"https://www.experienceperception.com/\"},{\"title\":\"Sci-Fi to Your Screen: User Interface Design Inspired by Futuristic Films\",\"url\":\"https://liistudio.com/sci-fi-ui-design-inspired-by-films/\"},{\"title\":\"Secret Headquarters Technology Design | PERCEPTION\",\"url\":\"https://www.experienceperception.com/work/secret-headquarters-technology/\"}]\n\nHere's a comprehensive overview of **Perception Design Studio**, their work on the Avengers and Marvel Cinematic Universe (MCU) UI design, typography, and their sci-fi interface design process:\n\n---\n\n## 🎬 Perception Design Studio — Marvel UI & Sci-Fi Interface Design\n\n### Who Is Perception?\n\nPerception is a New York-based visual effects studio specializing in UI/HUD design and title design for feature films. Founded in 2001 by former R/GA employees Jeremy Lasky and Danny Gonzalez, the studio is most notable for the creation of user interface/HUD designs and special visual effects sequences for a number of Marvel Studios films, as well as main-on-end title sequences for *The Avengers: Age of Ultron*, *Spider-Man: Homecoming*, and *Thor: Ragnarok*.\n\nPerception is an Emmy-nominated design lab pioneering the visionary process of \"Science Fiction Thinking\" to architect the future. They divide their time equally between the parallel worlds of science-fiction — working with trailblazing filmmakers — and science-fact — collaborating with the world's most innovative technology brands.\n\n---\n\n### 🦸 Work on the Avengers & Marvel Universe\n\nIf you've seen a Marvel movie in the past ten years, you've seen the work of NYC-based design firm Perception. They're the ones that designed the crazy holographic UIs used by Tony Stark, Black Panther, the Avengers, and others.\n\nPerception collaborates with Marvel Studios to conceptualize various pieces of advanced technology for the Avengers. One of the first sequences their team was asked to work on involved Steve Rogers (aka Captain America) becoming accustomed to the modern world. As he wanders the streets of New York, he walks past an Acura dealership with a sophisticated holographic or \"smart glass\" display. They explored numerous options to give director Joss Whedon a choice as to how advanced the display should be.\n\nThey were also tasked with creating new content for the main display onboard the Quinjet. Each of the concepts explored were designed to have a military-like utilitarian approach, as well as being created to match an array of existing displays, all shot practically.\n\nThey also explored various designs for the layout of Pepper Potts' cell phone featured in the climax of the film. These designs ranged from something that would feel like an evolution of Tony Stark's \"Stark-Tech\" phone to something more like a consumer-grade Stark Phone.\n\n---\n\n### 🖥️ Typography & Alien Interface Design\n\nFor films like *Secret Headquarters*, Perception's approach to typography is particularly noteworthy:\n\nMuch of the technology features flares of alien typography mixed in, since the tech is formed from the alien energy source. Their team developed this unique typography to be custom-made for the film. They experimented with various typefaces, and ultimately landed on a hexagonal-shaped text that reflects the patterns of the source. This typography can also be seen throughout the opening title sequence they designed.\n\nTheir final direction landed on mixing graphic glimpses of clips and images with mixtures of both English and alien typography.\n\n---\n\n### 🏆 Black Panther — Deep Collaboration & Design Challenges\n\nPerception was brought aboard early in the process for the specific purpose of consulting on potential areas of interest in science and technology. A brief consulting sprint evolved into 18 months of collaboration that included conceptual development and prototyping of various technologies for use in multiple sequences and scenarios.\n\nThe most central of these elements was the conceptualization and development of the vibranium sand interfaces throughout the film. Some of this work was used as design guidelines for various VFX houses while other elements were incorporated directly into the final shots by Perception. In addition to the various technologies, Perception worked closely on two special sequences — the opening 'history of Wakanda' prologue, and the main-on-end title sequence — both of which were based on the technological paradigm of vibranium sand.\n\nThey encountered various challenges on *Black Panther*, both conceptual and technical. An inspiring challenge was the need to design the most advanced technology in the Marvel Cinematic Universe, while conceptualizing something that had zero influence from any existing technologies.\n\n---\n\n### 🚀 The \"Science Fiction Thinking\" Design Philosophy\n\nAs Perception puts it: \"We use Science Fiction to perceive the world through the lens of what is possible instead of what currently exists.\" \"It allows us to mine our wildest imaginings of the future to analyze the present and consult with businesses to create and innovate.\"\n\nInterestingly, the firm has parlayed their sci-fi prowess into real-world work — collaborating with brands like General Motors. Perception is described by GM's Scott Martin (Creative Director, User Experience) as \"a Sci-Fi thinking design agency best known for their creative work within the Marvel universe,\" brought in to help create \"an exciting and theatrical cabin experience.\"\n\n---\n\n### 🌐 Sci-Fi UI Influencing the Real World\n\nThe UI for *Avengers: Infinity War* is very refined and considered. All the elements seem to be organized in a very structured manner and bound by clear rules, much like designing an operating system (OS).\n\nFilmmakers work with real designers — like Territory Studio, Perception, and G-Creative — to imagine UI that's sleek, smart, and rooted in how we want to interact with machines.\n\n---\n\nIn summary, **Perception** stands out as the go-to studio for Marvel's futuristic interface design, blending narrative storytelling, custom typography, character-driven aesthetics, and deep technical research to craft sci-fi UIs that not only serve the story but also inspire real-world innovation.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"03a1e1d2-1b6d-4555-a778-9f4b7dd563ac","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:17:11.161Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"66015413-8f28-408b-9a26-736eb8304ec7","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

{"parentUuid":"a3195a80-2328-49e0-8ed2-f1b1114b6da4","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"6c2d9219-4df4-497c-86ad-e2941100841a","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Ej6gG1895rLHZtvytbsQeD","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Neil Kellerhouse film title design typography Saul Bass cyberpunk sci-fi poster logomark\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"10 Typefaces From Movies | Blog | Domestika\",\"url\":\"https://www.domestika.org/en/blog/3589-10-typefaces-from-movies\"},{\"title\":\"Saul Bass - Wikipedia\",\"url\":\"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saul_Bass\"},{\"title\":\"Saul Bass design: How one designer rewrote the rules of film forever\",\"url\":\"https://elements.envato.com/learn/saul-bass-design\"},{\"title\":\"How Graphic Designer Saul Bass Revolutionized the Movie Poster - Dwell\",\"url\":\"https://www.dwell.com/article/saul-bass-movie-posters-05196b16\"},{\"title\":\"Neil Kellerhouse movie posters\",\"url\":\"https://www.cinematerial.com/artists/neil-kellerhouse-i1144\"},{\"title\":\"Saul Bass - Maestro of film title & poster design\",\"url\":\"https://www.orsonandwelles.co.uk/collections/saul-bass\"},{\"title\":\"Saul Bass — Art of the Title\",\"url\":\"https://www.artofthetitle.com/designer/saul-bass/\"},{\"title\":\"Neil Kellerhouse at Fonts In Use\",\"url\":\"https://fontsinuse.com/designers/1053/neil-kellerhouse\"},{\"title\":\"Saul Bass Didn't Just Design Movie Posters: Exploring His Iconic Logos and Timeless Influence\",\"url\":\"https://filmartgallery.com/blogs/news/saul-bass-didnt-just-design-movie-posters-exploring-his-iconic-logos-and-timeless-influence\"},{\"title\":\"Saul Bass: The man who changed graphic design\",\"url\":\"https://99designs.com/blog/famous-design/saul-bass-graphic-designer-of-a-century/\"}]\n\nHere's a synthesized overview of what the search results reveal about **Neil Kellerhouse**, **Saul Bass**, and the intersection of **film title design, typography, and logomark work**:\n\n---\n\n### 🎬 Neil Kellerhouse — Film Poster & Title Design\n\nNeil Kellerhouse, along with Michael Muller and Erik Buckham, created notable film poster work that has made a significant mark on popular culture in the realm of film graphic design. An archive of 45 movie posters attributed to Neil Kellerhouse has been catalogued on CineMaterial.\n\n---\n\n### 🎨 Saul Bass — The Godfather of Film Title Design & Typography\n\nSaul Bass (May 8, 1920 – April 25, 1996) was an American graphic designer and filmmaker, best known for his design of motion-picture title sequences, film posters, and corporate logos.\n\nA partnership with Alfred Hitchcock led to some of the most iconic Saul Bass artwork, including his development of **kinetic typography** for the opening sequences of *North by Northwest* (1959), *Vertigo* (1958), and *Psycho* (1960), a development which revolutionized credits.\n\nInstead of just providing names, Bass used striking graphics, clever typography, and abstract animation to create a narrative within the credits themselves, making them a key part of the film's storytelling.\n\n---\n\n### 🔤 Typography & Design Philosophy\n\nBass's \"kinetic-meets-constructivism\" typography is a Bass signature, and makes his posters instantly recognizable.\n\nBass's style was heavily influenced by modernist designer György Kepes and the Bauhaus principles — a design movement that emphasized simplicity and functionality. The Bauhaus philosophy was all about doing away with overly decorative elements, focusing instead on clean, geometric forms that served a clear purpose — a minimalist approach that sought to make design accessible, practical, and impactful without unnecessary complexity.\n\n---\n\n### 🔷 Logomark Design\n\nBass also designed some of the most iconic corporate logos in North America, including the original AT&T \"bell\" logo in 1969 and their later \"globe\" logo in 1983. He also designed Continental Airlines' 1968 \"jetstream\" logo and United Airlines' 1974 \"tulip\" logo, which became some of the most recognized logos of the era.\n\nHe believed that a logo wasn't just a symbol — it was a story in itself, one that could express a company's values and mission in the blink of an eye.\n\n---\n\n### 🚀 Enduring Influence on Sci-Fi / Modern Cinema\n\nInfused with psychological drama, the Saul Bass design legacy can still be seen in contemporary movie credits, movie poster designs, and other retro graphic design. His engrossing animation style continues to influence typographic title sequences across streaming and film.\n\nYou can see Bass's fingerprints in the striking opening sequences of modern films, where abstract visuals and kinetic typography capture the audience's attention from the very first frame.\n\n---\n\n**Note:** The search did not surface a specific project that directly links **Neil Kellerhouse** to a **cyberpunk or sci-fi** film title or poster with an explicit **Saul Bass stylistic** reference. If you're researching a specific film or project, feel free to share more details and I can refine the search!\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"cc73d477-7294-4545-bf81-2131de701953","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:17:34.071Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"a3195a80-2328-49e0-8ed2-f1b1114b6da4","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

Super Dev Resources\",\"url\":\"https://superdevresources.com/techno-sci-fi-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"20 Best Cyberpunk Fonts to Make Your Design Futuristic\",\"url\":\"https://din-studio.com/best-fonts/cyberpunk-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"38 Futuristic Cyberpunk Fonts To Give Your Works a Radical Edge | HipFonts\",\"url\":\"https://hipfonts.com/cyberpunk-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"27+ Best Cyberpunk Fonts for Radical Designs\",\"url\":\"https://justcreative.com/cyberpunk-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"32 Futuristic Fonts to Make Your Designs Pop | Figma\",\"url\":\"https://www.figma.com/resource-library/futuristic-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"Bold and Beautiful: 11+ Cyberpunk Fonts That Define the Future of Typography - Designertale\",\"url\":\"https://designertale.com/blog/cyberpunk-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"40 Cyberpunk Fonts to Transport You to the Future - Design Work Life\",\"url\":\"https://designworklife.com/cyberpunk-fonts-to-transport-you-to-the-future/\"},{\"title\":\"Cyberpunk Fonts - Futuristic and Edgy Styles\",\"url\":\"https://studio2am.co/collections/cyberpunk-fonts\"},{\"title\":\"30 Best Cyberpunk Fonts for an Edgy Design - Vandelay Design\",\"url\":\"https://www.vandelaydesign.com/cyberpunk-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"250+ Free Cyberpunk Fonts · 1001 Fonts\",\"url\":\"https://www.1001fonts.com/cyberpunk-fonts.html\"}]\n\nHere is a comprehensive overview of **sci-fi and cyberpunk typography**, broken down by font classification, with notes on emotional register:\n\n---\n\n## 🚀 Sci-Fi & Cyberpunk Typography: Font Classifications\n\n### 1. 🔷 Geometric Sans-Serif\nGeometric sans-serif fonts like **Orbitron** feature futuristic precision and symmetry. Orbitron is inspired by classic sci-fi and vintage digital displays — think video games, HUDs, and early tech interfaces.\n\n**Russo One** is a bold, geometric display font with rounded letterforms and a robust, industrial feel. Despite its slightly condensed proportions, it's highly legible, lending a powerful presence to any design, with a distinctive blend of sharp angles and smooth curves.\n\n**Emotional register:** Authority, precision, technological optimism, clean futurism.\n\n---\n\n### 2. 📐 Condensed Grotesque\n**Funnel Display** uses condensed proportions and sharp angles to create a strong visual impact. Its bold structure works well in tight spaces and commands attention in high-energy layouts.\n\n**Raskhal** is a condensed font offering a beautiful typographic connection for various design projects, also featured with retro-inspired letter design to help give a cool and bold look.\n\n**Emotional register:** Urgency, tension, gritty dystopia, high-impact headlines.\n\n---\n\n### 3. 🖨️ Monospace\n**Space Mono**, by Colophon Foundry, is a monospace (fixed-width) display type family with a retro-futuristic vibe. Its structured, uniform letterforms infuse both geometric and grotesque influences with qualities similar to headline typefaces of the 1960s like Microgramma and Eurostile, widely adopted in popular science fiction movies (*2001: A Space Odyssey*, *Star Trek*).\n\nThe monospace structure gives fonts like these a technical edge, making them surprisingly versatile — suitable for display and short body text in design systems.\n\n**Emotional register:** Cold rationality, machine intelligence, hacker culture, terminal aesthetics.\n\n---\n\n### 4. ✂️ Stencil\n**District** is a futuristic stencil sans-serif typeface. This minimalistic font is ideal for sci-fi movies, esports logos, video games, technology gadgets, branding, posters, presentations, and packaging. Thanks to its dynamic nature, District is very legible.\n\n**Wallpoet** captures the essence of vintage signage. The gaps in the forms resemble how letters are cut out from a stencil, giving it an industrial flair. Wallpoet is great for designers seeking a more rebellious and rough-around-the-edges aesthetic in their futuristic fonts.\n\n**Eternals** is a bold display font with a futuristic and space technology feel. Designed by Nauli Creative, this font features heavy, sturdy stenciled letterforms. It is suitable for use at large size for posters and headlines, perfect for music posters, game and movie titles as well as branding projects.\n\n**Emotional register:** Military grit, rebellion, industrial rawness, post-apocalyptic tension.\n\n---\n\n### 5. 🔲 Squarish / Techno (Cyberpunk-specific)\nFonts with squarish letterforms like **Neuropol** are perfect for cyberpunk aesthetics. Its squarish, rounded-corner design is based on popular techno fonts Microgramma and Chimes, with truncated horizontal strokes in some characters that emulate laser or vector beam traces, perfect for the cyberpunk theme.\n\n**Biotrons** is inspired by the core of cyberpunk — it aims to represent marginalized communities becoming renegades in a dystopian future. Its characteristics are sharp, pointed, and rough-shaped letters.\n\n**Emotional register:** Dystopian power, underground rebellion, neon-lit urban darkness.\n\n---\n\n### 6. 🎮 Pixel / Retro-Digital\n**Cyber Soulja** is a pixelated sans-serif typeface that recalls the 1980s and 1990s. Each letter is crafted with precision, capturing the retro aesthetic of classic arcade games. Its bold, blocky letters are reminiscent of the futuristic, sci-fi themes of the era, making it great for game design, titles, packaging, posters, and logos.\n\n**Emotional register:** Nostalgia, retro-futurism, lo-fi tech, digital subculture.\n\n---\n\n### 7. 💡 Emotional Register & Audience Considerations\nFonts aren't just text — they carry emotional weight and set the tone of the message. For instance, a font that vibrates with neon energy might captivate a younger, tech-savvy audience but overwhelm someone more accustomed to classical aesthetics.\n\nIf targeting a gaming community, opt for fonts with pixelated designs or glitch effects to echo digital environments. Conversely, a more general audience may prefer subtler, cleaner lines that still nod to the cyberpunk essence without being overpowering.\n\nFuturistic fonts offer a blend of high-tech and experimental elements. Most are sans-serif with geometric shapes, sharp angles, or sci-fi-inspired details, often built to shine on digital screens.\n\n---\n\n### Summary Table\n\n| Classification | Example Fonts | Emotional Register |\n|---|---|---|\n| Geometric Sans | Orbitron, Russo One | Precision, optimism, clean tech |\n| Condensed Grotesque | Funnel Display, Raskhal | Urgency, grit, dystopian edge |\n| Monospace | Space Mono, Exan-3 | Machine logic, hacker, terminal |\n| Stencil | District, Wallpoet, Eternals | Rebellion, industrial, raw power |\n| Squarish/Techno | Neuropol, Biotrons | Neon noir, cyberpunk underground |\n| Pixel/Retro-Digital | Cyber Soulja, Broken Console | Nostalgia, lo-fi, arcade culture |\n\nEach category carries its own distinct **emotional and cultural charge**, making font choice a critical storytelling tool in sci-fi and cyberpunk design.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"fddec983-c519-4b92-bdee-ebc4d1bcbc06","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:18:19.202Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"4d6c5209-279f-4624-b5e8-7094a740b376","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

{"parentUuid":"cb0027aa-c992-4062-99e2-bb48f7c83245","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"067b2f36-c97f-4651-8777-988c680233c0","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_016DUEVgva69VFWcahCoxdf5","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Eurostile Microgramma Bank Gothic sci-fi film typography history 2001 Space Odyssey Alien\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Iconic Sci-Fi Fonts from Classic Movies (and How to Choose Your Own)\",\"url\":\"https://blog.frame.io/2017/12/11/iconic-sci-fi-fonts-movies/\"},{\"title\":\"2001: A Space Odyssey | Typeset In The Future\",\"url\":\"https://typesetinthefuture.com/2014/01/31/2001-a-space-odyssey/\"},{\"title\":\"Typeset in the Future - TV Tropes\",\"url\":\"https://tvtropes.org/pmwiki/pmwiki.php/Main/TypesetInTheFuture\"},{\"title\":\"Fontspots: Eurostile | Typeset In The Future\",\"url\":\"https://typesetinthefuture.com/2014/11/29/fontspots-eurostile/\"},{\"title\":\"Typeset in the Future: Typography and Design in Science Fiction Movies - Dave Addey - Google Books\",\"url\":\"https://books.google.com/books/about/Typeset_in_the_Future.html?id=woFnDwAAQBAJ\"},{\"title\":\"Eurostile - Wikipedia\",\"url\":\"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostile\"},{\"title\":\"The Age of Plastic - 2001’s Zero Gravity Toilet Sign and its Typography\",\"url\":\"https://sites.google.com/site/theageofplastic3d/2001s-zero-gravity-toilet-sign-and-its-typography\"},{\"title\":\"Advanced Typography & Publication Design: Microgramma & Eurostile\",\"url\":\"http://sdesadvtype2013s1.blogspot.com/2013/03/microgramma-eurostile.html\"},{\"title\":\"Moon | Typeset In The Future\",\"url\":\"https://typesetinthefuture.com/2014/02/11/moon/\"},{\"title\":\"Think-Dash: 2001: A Space Odyssey\",\"url\":\"http://www.think-dash.com/2014/02/2001-space-odyssey.html\"}]\n\nHere is a comprehensive overview of the history and use of **Eurostile**, **Microgramma**, and **Bank Gothic** in sci-fi film typography, with a focus on *2001: A Space Odyssey*, *Alien*, and beyond:\n\n---\n\n## 🚀 Sci-Fi Film Typography: Eurostile, Microgramma & Bank Gothic\n\n### Origins of the Fonts\n\nMicrogramma and Eurostile are a pair of typefaces co-created by Italian designer **Aldo Novarese** during the mid-20th century. Novarese, while working at the Nebiolo foundry in Turin, designed Microgramma in **1952**, along with Alessandro Butti.\n\nAn uppercase-only, sans-serif font, Microgramma employed clean, rectangular letterforms capped off with rounded corners for a distinctive display font look, which mirrored and predicted much of what was to be considered modern and forward-looking over the next several decades.\n\nA decade after Novarese's involvement with Microgramma, Novarese designed **Eurostile** as a successor — adding lowercase letters, a bold condensed variant, and an ultra-narrow design he called Eurostile Compact, for a total of seven fonts. The design was completed in **1962** and renamed Eurostile.\n\nLike Microgramma, Eurostile is popular for headings and signs, especially in science fiction and media set in the 1960s and 70s. Its linear nature suggests modern architecture, with an appeal both technical and functional. The squarish superellipse shape evokes television screens from the 1950s and 1960s.\n\nAs for **Bank Gothic**: Bank Gothic is a sans-serif typeface designed by Morris Fuller Benton in **1930** for the American Type Foundry.\n\n---\n\n### The \"Typeset in the Future\" Trope\n\nEurostile, its precursor Microgramma, and the very similar Bank Gothic are sans-serif fonts used in many science fiction films to give a futuristic feel.\n\nIn fact, they have been used so often that the presence of these fonts in the credits or set design now actually helps viewers quickly understand the setting of the scene. They are most commonly used in their bold extended versions. Common uses of Eurostile (typically its extended font) include signs on the inside (and outside) of spaceships; important text on computer display screens; dramatic film titles; and overlays on positioning shots to establish the location and/or chronology of the scene.\n\n---\n\n### *2001: A Space Odyssey* (1968)\n\n*2001: A Space Odyssey* — Stanley Kubrick's 1968 sci-fi masterpiece — offers a zero-gravity toilet, emergency resuscitations, exploding bolts, and product placement aplenty. It's also the Ur Example of **Eurostile Bold Extended**'s regular appearance in spacecraft user interfaces.\n\nThe user interface for **HAL 9000** in *2001: A Space Odyssey* uses Eurostile Bold Extended, and is almost certainly the **Trope Maker** for this particular trope.\n\nEurostile Bold Extended appears on the receptionist's language buttons when the Doctor arrives on the space station — though it could just as easily be Eurostile's precursor, Microgramma. It's nigh-impossible to tell them apart.\n\nSurviving blueprints published in *2001: The Lost Science* actually make reference to Microgramma on the control panels, and given the widespread use of Letraset dry (rub-down) transfers for labelling everything on the ship, it seems reasonable that the 2001 designers also used Letraset for the lettering.\n\nBeyond Eurostile, Stanley Kubrick's favorite typeface was the sci-fi popular **Futura**, though he decided to go with **Gill Sans** for the typeface of choice when designing the title graphic for *2001: A Space Odyssey*. Futura can be found throughout Kubrick's *2001: A Space Odyssey* on title cards, computer screens, and the end credits — you can even spot the typeface on HAL 9000.\n\n---\n\n### *Alien* (1979)\n\nRidley Scott's *Alien* allegedly used a slight variation of **Futura** for the film's famous opening title sequence. Some, however, argue that it was in fact **Helvetica Black**, and not a variation of Futura. Whatever the case, it was a successful design.\n\n---\n\n### Other Notable Sci-Fi Uses\n\nEurostile is used extensively in science fiction movies, among them *2001: A Space Odyssey* (especially for HAL 9000), *Jetsons: The Movie*, *Moon*, *Back to the Future*, *Starship Troopers*, *The Bourne Supremacy*, *The Andromeda Strain*, and *Total Recall*.\n\nGerry Anderson made extensive use of **Microgramma** in his productions, starting with *Thunderbirds*. In particular, *Captain Scarlet and the Mysterons* uses Microgramma Medium Extended in its opening credits.\n\nIn *2001*'s sequel, *2010: The Year We Make Contact*, all the Russian signage aboard the Leonov is set in Eurostile Bold Extended, modified with Cyrillic letters.\n\nBank Gothic served as the typeface for the title graphic in Duncan Jones' critically acclaimed 2009 feature film debut *Moon*. Bank Gothic has long been considered Eurostile's great rival — the yang to Eurostile's yin — and *Moon* uses both fonts in quick succession for maximum futuristic effect.\n\nSince 1979's *Star Trek: The Motion Picture*, a variant of Microgramma/Eurostile has been the standard for the nameplates of spaceships in the Star Trek franchise. It appears from time to time in signage and computer screens as well, but ship hulls are where you're guaranteed to find it.\n\n---\n\n### Why These Fonts Work for Sci-Fi\n\nThere are a couple of reasons these typefaces work for futuristic design. First, they have a simplicity and uniformity one might attribute to the efficiency of an AI or other technologically advanced society. Secondly, simple sans-serif fonts tend to stand the test of time as they don't have any overtly ornate characteristics which may become dated.\n\nIn short, Eurostile, Microgramma, and Bank Gothic have become the *de facto* visual language of the future — so much so that their mere presence on screen instantly signals \"futuristic setting\" to audiences worldwide.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"7826c1f4-4c30-471c-bf54-77ea391ac00d","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:19:06.689Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"cb0027aa-c992-4062-99e2-bb48f7c83245","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

{"parentUuid":"2bf2a8a1-6267-4725-b1f2-b3c96e89d313","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"progress","data":{"type":"search_results_received","resultCount":10,"query":"logomark typography pairing rules tight tracking wide letterspacing stacked inline geometric mark relationship brand identity design"},"toolUseID":"srvtoolu_01QHVu1PPkVS6YxYsfM96Ymo","parentToolUseID":"toolu_01W39PazjMtiuw8bXQgwLyfz","uuid":"4933e3bc-d9e8-4fdf-97ef-3b65a14fea55","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:19:12.879Z","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}
{"parentUuid":"3cad589d-d908-45ff-9c82-173d39724d58","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"067b2f36-c97f-4651-8777-988c680233c0","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01W39PazjMtiuw8bXQgwLyfz","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"logomark typography pairing rules tight tracking wide letterspacing stacked inline geometric mark relationship brand identity design\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Types of logos & which to choose (no-BS guide)\",\"url\":\"https://cultmethod.com/articles/types-of-logos/\"},{\"title\":\"Why Tracking and Kerning Matter in Logo Typography?\",\"url\":\"https://www.logodesign.net/blog/tracking-kerning-logo-typography/\"},{\"title\":\"How to Balance Your Logos: Pairing your Logomark & Logotype Using Grids - Akrivi\",\"url\":\"https://www.akrivi.io/learn/how-to-balance-logos\"},{\"title\":\"Wordmark Logo Design: A Beginners Guide (With Examples) - Looka\",\"url\":\"https://looka.com/blog/wordmark-logo-design/\"},{\"title\":\"Typography | Brand Guidelines | Illinois\",\"url\":\"https://brand.illinois.edu/visual-identity/typography\"},{\"title\":\"Typography logo examples: 50+ brands that nailed it | Graphic design tips\",\"url\":\"https://icons8.com/blog/articles/typography-logo-design/\"},{\"title\":\"The Role of Typography in Branding | Insights | Little\",\"url\":\"https://www.littleonline.com/insights/the-role-of-typography-in-branding/\"},{\"title\":\"Logo Design: Understanding Logomarks, Wordmarks, Submarks, and More\",\"url\":\"https://www.thedigitalmediateacher.com/blog/logo-design-understanding-logomarks-wordmarks-submarks-and-more\"},{\"title\":\"Logo Design And Branding: The 2026 Guide To Visual Identity\",\"url\":\"https://inkbotdesign.com/logo-design-rules\"},{\"title\":\"The Difference Between Logomark, Logotype & Combination Mark Design\",\"url\":\"https://www.kimbodesign.ca/difference-between-logomark-logotype-combination-mark-design/\"}]\n\nHere is a comprehensive synthesis of key principles around **logomark and typography pairing rules** — covering tracking, letterspacing, stacked/inline layouts, geometric mark relationships, and brand identity design:\n\n---\n\n## 🔤 Logomark & Typography Pairing: Rules & Best Practices\n\n---\n\n### 1. Understanding Tracking vs. Kerning in Logo Typography\n\nThere's a distinction between \"tracking\" (letter-spacing) and \"kerning.\" Letter-spacing refers to the spacing between all letters — it can be **tight** (letters close together, like the FedEx logo) or **loose** (letters further apart, like the Creme logo).\n\nTracking deals with the overall density of a word by uniformly altering space between all characters, while kerning targets specific letter pairs — adjusting for visual inconsistencies rather than global spacing.\n\n---\n\n### 2. What Tight vs. Wide Tracking Communicates\n\n**Tight spacing** in logos feels intense, energetic, or aggressive — making it a strong choice for tech startups or athletic brands looking to project drive and momentum.\n\nOn the luxury end, \"the tracking is impossibly tight because luxury brands know scarcity applies to letter spacing too.\"\n\nConversely, **loose tracking** conveys a premium automotive or high-end feel — \"loose enough for premium automotive feels but tight enough not to look like a tech startup.\"\n\n---\n\n### 3. The Logomark–Logotype Relationship\n\nWhen designers ask how to \"balance a logo,\" they're usually referring to the process of **pairing a logomark (the symbol) with a logotype (the text)** in a clean, visually harmonious way.\n\nA **combination mark** is a logo that includes both a logomark (symbol) and a logotype (text), designed to work together as one unit. These are flexible because the text and symbol can sometimes be used separately depending on the branding need, and are very common in professional branding because they provide the most versatility.\n\n---\n\n### 4. Stacked vs. Inline (Horizontal) Lockup Rules\n\nThe two most common lockup types are: **Horizontal Lockup** (the logomark is placed beside the logotype) and **Vertical Lockup** (the logomark is placed above the logotype).\n\nFor **horizontal lockups**, a grid-based method focuses on harmonious spacing: position the logomark to the left and the logotype to its right. Draw guide lines across the top and bottom of the logotype, then extend them to encompass the mark — using these as alignment guides to position the mark perfectly in place.\n\nFor **vertical (stacked) lockups**, duplicate the logomark left and right edge-to-edge as temporary guides, then scale the logotype to fit exactly between the outer ends — this aligns the text width **optically** with the mark.\n\n---\n\n### 5. Stacked Text Legibility Rules\n\nWhen using **stacked text** in a logo, legibility is paramount. For example, Uniqlo breaks its six-letter name into three letters per line to form a square shape — but you should make sure your company name is short enough to stack and that the meaning doesn't get lost across two lines.\n\n---\n\n### 6. Geometric Marks & Typography Pairing\n\nIn terms of type personality, **geometric sans-serifs** signal modernity, tech-savviness, and efficiency — making them natural pairings for geometric logomarks.\n\nA typeface like Montserrat, for instance, \"has a geometric, modernist look and feel, with straight lines and circular forms\" — making it highly compatible with geometric or abstract marks.\n\nA healthcare brand might pair a calming, modern logo with easy-to-read sans-serif fonts, while a tech start-up might use **bold, geometric typefaces** to create a sense of energy and innovation.\n\n---\n\n### 7. Typography as a Brand Identity Tool\n\nFrom point size to kerning and leading, typography affects how easily content can be read and how it aligns with the brand's identity. In brand design, typography isn't just about appearance — **it's a strategic tool**.\n\nUnderstanding logotypes teaches that typography is not just functional — it is expressive. Every typeface, curve, and letterform adjustment plays a role in shaping a brand's personality.\n\nSpacing also impacts the words' readability, subsequently **affecting the speed at which your audience can recognize and recall your brand**.\n\n---\n\n### 8. Logomarks for Established vs. New Brands\n\nSymbol logos are long-term plays. Unless you are a major brand or have hundreds of millions of dollars to spend on advertising, it will take a long time until people recognize your symbol and associate it with your brand — so you'll want to use it in conjunction with a wordmark as much as possible.\n\nA well-designed combination mark offers flexibility for the use of either or both the brand name and/or the symbol — effective with both elements together or separated, offering wider flexibility for branding.\n\n---\n\n### Summary Table\n\n| Element | Tight Tracking | Wide Tracking |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Brand Feel** | Luxury, intense, energetic | Airy, premium, open |\n| **Best for** | Tech, athletic, luxury fashion | Automotive, wellness, lifestyle |\n| **Logomark pairing** | Compact/inline lockup | Stacked/spaced lockup |\n| **Type style** | Geometric sans, condensed | Humanist sans, extended |\n\nThese rules together ensure that your logomark and typographic logotype work as a **unified, intentional brand identity system** rather than disconnected elements.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"b92fd73e-14b7-4e0a-b7a6-e57c3082533e","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:19:47.574Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"3cad589d-d908-45ff-9c82-173d39724d58","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

search results for query: \"retro-futurism typography VHS CRT aesthetic Japanese city pop fonts Akira title card Ghost in the Shell credits typeface\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Ghost In The Shell | Fonts\",\"url\":\"https://www.fontspace.com/category/futuristic,retro\"}]\n\nHere's a rich breakdown of what the search uncovered on **retro-futurism typography, VHS/CRT aesthetics, Japanese city pop fonts, and iconic anime title cards** like *Akira* and *Ghost in the Shell*:\n\n---\n\n## 🎌 Akira Title Card Typography\n\nThe *Akira* logo has one of the most fascinating typographic histories in anime/manga:\n\n- What most people recognize as the Akira logo is actually the title on the cover of the paperback collection published by Kodansha from 1984.\n\n- The earliest issues (1–35) used \"a bold textbook typeface for photo-typesetting,\" identified as **石井太教科書体 BTA (Ishii Futo Kyokashotai)** from the Shaken (写研) foundry, which specialized in phototypesetting.\n\n- On the cover of issue 36, the title shifted to Latin letters from **Broadway**, which was immediately replaced by **ITC Busorama** (issues 37–48), described as \"reminiscent of the New York skyscrapers built in the 20s and 30s.\"\n\n- From issue 49 to 71, the title returned to Japanese katakana, with calligraphic lettering done by **Hiroshi Hirata**, another manga artist.\n\n- For the final batch of issues (72–120), Otomo settled on a bold weight of **Futura**, declaring: \"We had finally found the title style that suited Akira's story.\"\n\n- Otomo and his design office, Pencil Studio, also experimented with futuristic typefaces such as **Checkmate** and **Earth** during their cover research.\n\n- He eventually settled on condensed sans-serif capitals for a \"majestic, yet simple and slightly coarse image.\" It is unclear if he used a trimmed **Impact**, or something close like **Schmalfette Grotesk** — the *Akira Club* book notes \"Impact with the top and bottom cut,\" though the K and R don't seem to fit perfectly.\n\n---\n\n## 👻 Ghost in the Shell Typography\n\n- The *Ghost in the Shell* typeface is rooted in a **science fiction/futuristic theme**, more specifically the **cyberpunk** subgenre and the futurism movement.\n\n- The font **Mitashi** was designed as a direct tribute, \"inspired by the futuristic vibe of cyberpunk, specifically drawing from the *Ghost in the Shell* universe,\" meant to evoke the sleek, high-tech aesthetic of a cyber-enhanced world.\n\n---\n\n## 📺 VHS & CRT Aesthetic Fonts\n\n- Fonts like **Workbench** feature variable font technology allowing dynamic adjustments to scanlines and pixel bleed — two features directly inspired by **CRT monitors** — with blocky, pixelated letterforms, sharp corners, and a distinctly retro digital aesthetic.\n\n- Other typefaces are \"inspired by 80s computer system aesthetics and CRT displays,\" with packs that include **Space Invaders** characters and template PSDs with **CRT glitch effects** for text and backgrounds.\n\n- **Sixtyfour** pays homage to the **Commodore 64** computer by capturing the essence of its iconic display font, best suited for video games, digital art, headlines, and logos.\n\n---\n\n## 🌆 Retro-Futurism Typography: General Principles\n\n- At its core, retro-futurism embraces a visual narrative that combines **technological optimism with vintage elements**, drawing from iconic sci-fi aesthetics of the 60s, 70s, and 80s — incorporating smooth curves, bold geometric shapes, neon colors, and metallic textures.\n\n- Fonts play a critical role in retro-futurism by **bridging the gap between past and future**, often combining bold, experimental letterforms with a retro twist, making them ideal for futuristic yet vintage-inspired designs.\n\n- Retro-futurism also embraces **neon and metallic-inspired fonts** — typefaces that mimic the glow of neon lights or the sleekness of metallic surfaces add a futuristic element while remaining rooted in retro aesthetics.\n\n- **Rubik 80s Fade** captures the essence of 1980s retro-futurism in its playful aesthetic, created using NaN Glyph filters as a modification of the original Rubik font, with the designer aiming to evoke the visual language of **80s graphic design with halftone textures**.\n\n---\n\n## 🔤 Notable Retro-Futurist & Cyberpunk Typefaces to Explore\n\n| Font | Style / Notes |\n|---|---|\n| **Futura (Bold)** | Final *Akira* logo font |\n| **ITC Busorama** | Used in *Akira* manga covers |\n| **Mitashi** | *Ghost in the Shell*-inspired cyberpunk font |\n| **Workbench** | CRT scanline variable font |\n| **Sixtyfour** | Commodore 64 homage |\n| **Rubik 80s Fade** | 80s halftone retro-futurism |\n| **Audiowide** | LED/segmented display nod with wide, blocky letterforms and rounded corners giving a distinct retro-futuristic feel |\n| **Michroma** | Geometric sans-serif drawing inspiration from technical drawings and digital interfaces, with sharp structured forms and a distinctly futuristic tone |\n\nWhether you're designing anime-inspired title cards, synthwave visuals, city pop album art, or VHS-era aesthetics, this typographic universe is rich with history and creative possibility! 🎴📼\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown {"parentUuid":"645e632a-0bf9-44e7-ab74-348871f33faa","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"be8ef2e2-8dff-48ad-9d4b-052802914edd","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01JyqCbKcdcJK6qtTvUzd7F1","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"variable fonts custom typeface brand identity system sub-brands Google Material Apple SF gaming studio faction logos logo family\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Fonts in famous logos: 10 examples where typefaces define a brand identity | Creative Bloq\",\"url\":\"https://www.creativebloq.com/design/fonts-typography/fonts-in-famous-logos-10-examples-where-typefaces-define-a-brand-identity\"},{\"title\":\"Custom Font & Brand Identity | French Type Design foundry 🔥 We design fonts for blazing hot projects!\",\"url\":\"https://blazetype.eu/blog/custom-font-brand-identity\"},{\"title\":\"Typography Trends: Variable Fonts for Responsive Design – New Target\",\"url\":\"https://www.newtarget.com/web-insights-blog/variable-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"Variable Fonts\",\"url\":\"https://v-fonts.com/\"},{\"title\":\"Typefaces (Fonts) | Visual Identity System | Branding and Identity Guidelines | Brandeis University\",\"url\":\"https://www.brandeis.edu/brand/visual-identity-system/typefaces-fonts.html\"},{\"title\":\"15 Brands That Created Their Own Custom Typeface | World Branding Forum\",\"url\":\"https://brandingforum.org/general/15-brands-that-created-their-own-custom-typeface\"},{\"title\":\"Mastering Brand Typography and Fonts - Clash Graphics\",\"url\":\"https://www.clashgraphics.com/printing-tips/mastering-brand-typography-and-fonts/\"},{\"title\":\"Open Source Brand Fonts - Google Design\",\"url\":\"https://design.google/library/open-source-custom-fonts-google\"},{\"title\":\"Brand Typography: Examples to Inspire Your Visual Identity | Canny Creative\",\"url\":\"https://www.canny-creative.com/brand-typography/\"},{\"title\":\"The Top 41 Logo Fonts and How to Choose the Perfect One | Looka\",\"url\":\"https://looka.com/blog/choosing-and-using-your-logo-font/\"}]\n\nHere is a comprehensive overview of the intersection of **variable fonts, custom typefaces, brand identity systems, sub-brands, and logo families** — drawing from multiple authoritative sources:\n\n---\n\n## 🔤 Variable Fonts & Brand Identity Systems\n\nVariable fonts are an advancement in font technology that allows a single font file to encompass multiple styles and variations. In traditional typography, if you wanted a typeface in regular, bold, and italic, you'd need to load three separate font files. In contrast, a variable font file can include all these styles (and many more variations) in one package — essentially, one font file behaves like an entire font family.\n\nThis is one reason we're seeing large brands commission custom variable fonts for their identities. They get a bespoke typeface that's adaptable for all uses — print, web, mobile app, TV screen, etc. — without having to design multiple separate font families.\n\nVariable fonts provide more design flexibility, allowing small changes in typography to enhance brand identity across different platforms without introducing new type families. They also cater to responsive typography by adjusting to small screens, enhancing readability on large screens, adapting to orientation changes, and meeting individual user preferences to fulfill accessibility requirements.\n\n---\n\n## 🍎 Apple's San Francisco (SF) — A Multi-Variant Type System\n\nApple's official typeface is San Francisco, which appears on screens and Apple keyboards. Easy to read and language-adaptive, San Francisco comprises up to nine weights (including italics) and several extensions.\n\nThe San Francisco typeface features three variants: SF Pro, SF Compact, and SF Mono. Besides those, Apple's New York typeface is a serif companion for San Francisco. Additionally, script extensions that support other languages include SF Arabic, SF Armenian, SF Georgian, and SF Hebrew.\n\nSF Arabic is an extension of Apple's San Francisco type series, used as the Arabic system font on Apple platforms. It is \"a contemporary interpretation of the Naskh style with a rational and flexible design.\"\n\nApple's custom font, San Francisco, is a sleek, sans-serif typeface that quietly does its job without demanding attention. On your iPhone or MacBook, it just works. It's crisp, clear, and readable at any size. It reflects Apple's whole design philosophy: keep things simple, put the product first, and make everything feel seamless.\n\n---\n\n## 🔍 Google — Product Sans, Noto, and an Open Font Ecosystem\n\nProduct Sans is a geometric sans serif typeface custom-made for Google as part of its corporate branding. The typeface is exclusive and cannot be used outside of the brand.\n\nGoogle uses its own font Product Sans for most of its typography needs, and the Google logo is no exception, although the font was modified slightly. The smooth curves give it a friendly and approachable but balanced feel, and the clean lines reflect Google's aim for simplicity and accessibility in its user-centric UI and UX design. Product Sans is also used for the logo of Google's parent company Alphabet.\n\nGoogle's own font superfamily, Noto, was developed as a set of fonts for all languages — a major feat considering the vast differences in how text appears in different writing systems. It uses compatible heights and stroke thicknesses with the specific goal of eliminating \"tofu,\" those annoying little empty rectangles that show up when a glyph is missing from a font. There are 210 individual font families in the Noto superfamily, covering thousands of languages and 162 writing systems.\n\nToday, there are over 17 open-source Google Font families commissioned by large brands, creative agencies, nonprofits, and even government organizations. These professionally developed fonts are available for anyone to use in their own projects, or as inspiration for new designs.\n\n---\n\n## 🎮 Gaming Studios, Faction Logos & Logo Families\n\nTake The Northern Block, based in the North East of England. Founded by Jonathan Hill in 2006, the studio has built a reputation for clean and tech-forward fonts used by creatives, brands, and even **video game developers**. Their work spans Latin and multi-script typefaces, and their commitment to function, clarity, and innovation has earned them international recognition.\n\nFor gaming studios and sub-brand/faction logo systems, typeface families are a crucial tool for visual cohesion across multiple sub-identities:\n\nA font will serve to homogenize the different logos used within the same company. The most obvious example is the acquisition by a parent company of a new subsidiary and the harmonization of the new identities acquired.\n\nTypeface and font development can take many months, as the scope of the work can be quite massive: several weights, several styles, a large character set, OpenType functions, ligatures, or contextual variants. It is no longer a tool but heavy artillery dedicated to communication.\n\n---\n\n## 🏢 Sub-Brands & Custom Typefaces in Large Brand Systems\n\nThe IBM Plex superfamily encompasses mono, sans serif, serif, and condensed versions, each designed to convey distinct characteristics while maintaining the integrity of the IBM brand identity.\n\nGitHub's custom fonts Mona Sans and Hubot Sans are available on fonts.google.com. Designed together with Degarism, both are variable fonts.\n\nAlthough custom typefaces can be expensive, they are a worthy investment so brands can safeguard their business and corporate identity. Generic typefaces require businesses to pay licensing rights whenever they are in use, not to mention risking copyright infringement.\n\nSegoe, designed by Steve Matteson for Monotype, is now a registered trademark of Microsoft. Along with the Segoe UI sub-family, it's used for everything from the Microsoft logo to brand communications and as the default operating system font in various Microsoft products.\n\n---\n\n## ✅ Key Takeaway\n\nTypography is foundational to brand identity systems, especially when managing sub-brands, faction logos, or logo families across a universe of products. Brand typography is essential for conveying a brand's unique identity and personality, affecting brand recognition, and establishing a visual symphony that engages consumers. Understanding different font categories and selecting the suitable typeface is crucial for matching a brand's personality and message. Consistency, readability, and a well-established typographic hierarchy are vital in typography to maintain user engagement and enhance brand recognition across various platforms and mediums.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"79711f20-0f6d-49ac-a5c6-84e7e327f48c","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:21:35.650Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"645e632a-0bf9-44e7-ab74-348871f33faa","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}

{"parentUuid":"5784a015-560a-484b-a4f8-c40bbcc06c80","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"be8ef2e2-8dff-48ad-9d4b-052802914edd","agentId":"ac62ac50d4010ee8d","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01UoHcWzLbCo97m16LdJAbLm","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Neil Kellerhouse Under the Skin Social Network poster typography font choices design process\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"The fonts of David Fincher\",\"url\":\"https://www.pixartprinting.co.uk/blog/fonts-david-fincher/\"},{\"title\":\"Graphic designer Neil Kellerhouse talks through his process designing the poster for Jonathan Glazer’s ‘Under the Skin’ - FAN THE FIRE\",\"url\":\"http://fanthefiremagazine.com/film/graphic-designer-neil-kellerhouse-talks-through-the-process-designing-the-poster-for-jonathan-glazers-under-the-skin/\"},{\"title\":\"The Social Network - Fonts In Use\",\"url\":\"https://fontsinuse.com/uses/9/the-social-network\"},{\"title\":\"Neil Kellerhouse at Fonts In Use\",\"url\":\"https://fontsinuse.com/designers/1053/neil-kellerhouse\"},{\"title\":\"neil kellerhouse | Tumblr\",\"url\":\"https://www.tumblr.com/search/neil+kellerhouse/recent\"},{\"title\":\"The Social Network poster font - forum | dafont.com\",\"url\":\"https://www.dafont.com/forum/read/7675/the-social-network-poster-font\"},{\"title\":\"Brad Pitt Was Almost in 'Under the Skin' and 9 Kellerhouse Posters\",\"url\":\"https://www.comingsoon.net/movies/news/589616-brad-pitt-almost-skin-9-kellerhouse-posters\"},{\"title\":\"The film poster designs of Neil Kellerhouse. Everything he touches turns to art. Check out more of his work online HERE. – @bicephalypictures on Tumblr\",\"url\":\"https://www.tumblr.com/bicephalypictures/85825548313/the-film-poster-designs-of-neil-kellerhouse\"},{\"title\":\"Check Out Kellerhouse's Minimalistic Set of 'Under the Skin' Posters | FirstShowing.net\",\"url\":\"https://www.firstshowing.net/2014/check-out-kellerhouses-minimalistic-set-of-under-the-skin-posters/\"},{\"title\":\"35 Neil Kellerhouse ideas | commercial art, movie posters ...\",\"url\":\"https://www.pinterest.com/shotsofnothing/neil-kellerhouse/\"}]\n\nHere's a summary of what the search results reveal about **Neil Kellerhouse**, his work on *Under the Skin*, *The Social Network*, and his broader design process:\n\n---\n\n### 🎨 Neil Kellerhouse — Designer Overview\n\nFamed for his work on *The Girl with the Dragon Tattoo*, *I'm Still Here*, and *The Social Network*, graphic designer Neil Kellerhouse was asked by director Jonathan Glazer to do the advertising for the thriller *Under the Skin*.\n\nKellerhouse is known in Hollywood for his work on *The Revenant*, *Under the Skin*, and *Gone Girl*, among others.\n\n---\n\n### 🎬 *The Social Network* — Typography & Font Choices\n\n*The Social Network* film uses **Klavika** (along with **Futura**) for its logo and promotional graphics.\n\nThe Facebook logo itself is a judicious modification of Eric Olson's Klavika, with tighter spacing and some wider letterforms. In the same way that Facebook represents the culture and technology of a decade, Klavika does the same for typeface design — well crafted but blatantly simple, monolinear, square, and nearly devoid of calligraphic ancestry.\n\nIt's an expected and obvious choice, but it works well.\n\nAlong with the art for Fincher's *Dragon Tattoo*, Kellerhouse \"sort of set the stage for the rise in massive type over a film protagonist's face\" with his art for *The Social Network*.\n\n---\n\n### 🌌 *Under the Skin* — Design Process\n\nWanting to do something \"more than just a pretty picture,\" Kellerhouse wasn't afraid to fail a lot before they finally settled on the finished design.\n\nKellerhouse raced through an exhaustive archive of design iterations for *Under the Skin*'s poster.\n\nA set of minimalistic posters was designed by Neil Kellerhouse — the same artist/designer responsible for Fincher's outstanding *Girl with the Dragon Tattoo* promo art.\n\nA24 commissioned Kellerhouse to make posters for Jonathan Glazer's *Under the Skin*, which stars Scarlett Johansson as an alien in human form roaming through Scotland.\n\n---\n\n### 🔤 David Fincher's Typographic Legacy (Context)\n\nSomeone once described David Fincher's collection of opening credits as \"representing a genuine renaissance of this form of graphic and typographic design in cinema.\"\n\n---\n\n**Note:** The *Fan the Fire* article (source 2) appears to be a detailed interview where Kellerhouse specifically talks through his *Under the Skin* design process step by step, but the full content of the article was not accessible in the search results. You may want to visit [fanthefiremagazine.com](http://fanthefiremagazine.com/film/graphic-designer-neil-kellerhouse-talks-through-the-process-designing-the-poster-for-jonathan-glazers-under-the-skin/) directly for the complete in-depth breakdown of his typography and font choices.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"5e53cc2c-f80a-49fc-871a-f9de22b23e06","timestamp":"2026-03-14T07:22:02.433Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"5784a015-560a-484b-a4f8-c40bbcc06c80","userType":"external","cwd":"/Users/zksoju/Documents/GitHub/loa-constructs","sessionId":"baf9f486-c9af-492f-b21b-979b577ec1e7","version":"2.1.76","gitBranch":"main","slug":"modular-brewing-hedgehog"}
